∎

5.2.
Asymptotics of PAM started from constant initial data

In this section, we reveal how the solution of the parabolic Anderson model started from constant initial data is related to the largest point of the spectrum of Anderson Hamiltonian. We achieve this goal in Lemma
5.7
and
5.8
. It is worthwhile to note that similar claims have been proved for
d=2
case in
[
KPvZ20
]
. Showing those results for
d=3
needs new estimates which are provided in Lemma
5.4
and Proposition
5.5
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Recall
\mathds{Q}^{x,y}_{L,\epsilon}
and the diffusion
X
from Section
2
. Furthermore, recall
\mathscr{D}^{y}_{L,\epsilon}
defined in terms of
Z^{y}_{L,\epsilon}
,
Y^{y}_{L,\epsilon}
and
\eta
from Section
4
. Then, we have

(5.15)

\displaystyle\mathds{Q}^{x,y}_{L,\epsilon}(X{[0,t]}\not\subset Q^{y}_{L})\leq C\exp\left(Ct\mathfrak{a}_{\epsilon}(\log L)^{2}-\frac{L^{2}}{Ct}\right),

and

(5.16)

\mathds{E}_{\mathds{Q}^{x,y}_{L,\epsilon}}\left[\mathds{1}_{X[0,t]\not\subset Q^{y}_{r},X[0,t]\subset Q^{y}_{L}}\cdot\mathscr{D}^{y}_{L,\epsilon}(0,t)\right]\leq C\exp\left(C\mathfrak{a}_{\epsilon}^{\aleph+1}(\log L)^{2\aleph+2}-\frac{r^{2}}{Ct}\right)

where
\mathfrak{a}_{\epsilon}
is same as in the one defined in (
5.4
).

Proof.

In what follows, we use the upper bound on
\|Z^{y}_{L,\epsilon}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
,
\|Y^{y}_{L,\epsilon}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha}}
and
\eta^{y}_{L,\epsilon}
from Proposition
5.3
to derive upper and lower bound on
\mathscr{D}^{y}_{L,\epsilon}
.
Combining (
5.14
) with the definition of
\mathscr{D}^{y}_{L,\epsilon}
(
3.3
), for
L>1
,
s,t\geq 0
and
s<t
, we have

(5.17)

\displaystyle e^{-C\mathfrak{a}_{\epsilon}^{\aleph+1}(t-s)(\log L)^{2\aleph+2}}\leq\mathds{1}_{X{[s,t]}\subset Q^{y}_{L}}\cdot\mathscr{D}^{y}_{L,\epsilon}(s,t)\leq e^{C\mathfrak{a}_{\epsilon}^{\aleph+1}(t-s)(\log L)^{2\aleph+2}},

where
C>0
is an absolute constant and
\mathfrak{a}_{\epsilon}
is same as in (
5.4
). To complete the proof of the inequalities in (
5.15
) and (
5.17
), we further need bound on the transition probability of the diffusion
X
as defined in (
3.4
). We derive those in the following using the estimates of Theorem
4.14
.
Recall the upper and lower bound on the transition kernel
\Gamma_{t}^{L}(x,y)
from (
4.27
) and (
4.28
) respectively. We may bound
\|U_{L,\epsilon}\|=\|Z_{L,\epsilon}+Y_{L,\epsilon}\|_{\infty}
by
\|Z_{L,\epsilon}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}+\|Y_{L,\epsilon}\|_{2\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
which is further bounded above by
C\mathfrak{a}_{\epsilon}(\log L)^{2}
due to (
5.14
). As a result, we get for
L>r>e
and
L,r\in\mathds{N}

(5.18)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{L}_{t}(x,y)\geq\frac{1}{t^{d/2}}\exp\left(-C\mathfrak{a}_{\epsilon}(\log L)^{2}(1+\frac{r^{2}}{t})\right)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{L}_{t}(x,y)\leq p_{t}(x,y)e^{\mathfrak{a}_{\epsilon}(\log L)^{2}},

where
p_{t}
is the transition density of the
d-
dimensional Brownian motion. Note that
\mathds{Q}^{x,\epsilon}_{L,y}(X{[0,t]}\not\subset Q^{y}_{L})
is bounded above by
\mathbb{P}\big(\sup_{s\in[0,t]}|X_{s}-x|\geq L/2\big)
. Corollary
4.15
bounds the last probability by
C\exp(Ct\|Z_{L,\epsilon}+Y_{L,\epsilon}\|_{\infty}-\frac{L^{2}}{CT})
. Due to Proposition
5.3
, we may bound
\|Z_{L,\epsilon}+Y_{L,\epsilon}\|_{\infty}
by
C\mathfrak{a}_{\epsilon}(\log L)^{2}
. Plugging this into the bound
C\exp(Ct\|Z_{L,\epsilon}+Y_{L,\epsilon}\|_{\infty}-\frac{L^{2}}{CT})
yields the inequality (
5.15
). To prove (
5.16
), we first apply (
5.17
) to obtain

\mathds{E}_{\mathds{Q}^{x,y}_{L,\epsilon}}\left[\mathds{1}_{X[0,t]\not\subset Q^{y}_{r},X[0,t]\subset Q^{y}_{L}}\cdot\mathscr{D}^{y}_{L,\epsilon}(0,t)\right]\leq\mathds{Q}^{x,y}_{L,\epsilon}(X{[0,t]}\not\subset Q^{y}_{L})e^{C\mathfrak{a}_{\epsilon}^{\aleph+1}(t-s)(\log L)^{2\aleph+2}}.

Substituting (
5.15
) into the right hand side of the above display yields (
5.16
).
∎

Now we use the bounds of Lemma
5.4
to upper and lower bound of the solution of PAM (
2.1
)
started from constant initial data. Our proof ideas will be similar in spirit to
[
KPvZ20
, Lemma 3.2]
.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
L>1
. Recall that
u^{\mathds{1},y}_{L}
is the solution of PAM restricted on box
Q^{y}_{L}
under Dirichlet boundary condition started from constant initial data
\mathds{1}
. Then we have

(5.19)

u^{\mathds{1},y}_{L}(t,x)\leq C\exp\Big(t\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{L})+C\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{\aleph+1}(\log L)^{2\aleph+2}\Big),

where
\mathfrak{a}_{0}:=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathfrak{a}_{\epsilon}
. Moreover, for
t>\delta>1

(5.20)

\displaystyle u^{\mathds{1},y}_{L}(t,x)

\displaystyle\geq\exp\left(-C\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{\aleph+1}\delta(\log L)^{2\aleph+2}-\frac{C\mathfrak{a}_{0}(\log L)^{2}r^{2}}{\delta}+(t-\delta)\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{r})\right)

(5.21)

\displaystyle-\exp\left(C\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{2\aleph+1}t(\log L)^{2\aleph+2}-\frac{L^{2}}{C\delta}\right)

where
\aleph
is the same constant as in Proposition
5.3
.

Proof.

We start by writing that

(5.22)

u^{\mathds{1},y}_{L}(t,x)=\mathds{E}_{\mathds{Q}^{x,y}_{L}}\left[\mathscr{D}^{y}_{L}(0,t)\mathds{1}_{X[0,t]\subset Q^{y}_{L}}\right]=\int_{Q^{y}_{L}}u^{x,y}_{L}(t,z)dz.

where
u^{x,y}_{r}(t,z):=u^{\delta_{x},y}_{L}(t,z)
. Note that
u^{x,y}_{r}(t,z):=u^{\delta_{x},y}_{L}(t,z)=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}u^{\psi^{x}_{\epsilon},y}_{L}(t,z)
where
\psi^{x}_{\epsilon}(z):=\psi_{\epsilon}(z-x)\in C^{\infty}(Q_{L})
(see
[
Lab19
]
). For
\delta\in(1,t),\epsilon\in[0,1]
and
0<r<L
, we have

(5.23)

\displaystyle u^{x,y}_{r}(t,z)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\mathds{E}_{\mathds{Q}^{x,y}_{r,\epsilon}}\left[\mathscr{D}^{y}_{r,\epsilon}(0,t)\psi^{x}_{\epsilon}(X_{t})\mathds{1}_{X[0,t]\subset Q^{y}_{r}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}e^{C\mathfrak{a}^{\aleph+1}_{\epsilon}\delta(\log r)^{2\aleph+2}}\mathds{E}_{\mathds{Q}^{x,y}_{r,\epsilon}}\left[\mathscr{D}^{y}_{r,\epsilon}(0,t-\delta)\mathds{1}_{X[0,t-\delta]\subset Q^{y}_{r}}\mathds{E}_{\mathds{Q}^{x,y}_{r,\epsilon}}[\psi^{x}_{\epsilon}(X_{t})|X_{t-\delta}]\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}e^{C\mathfrak{a}^{\aleph+1}_{\epsilon}\delta(\log r)^{2\aleph+2}}\mathds{E}_{\mathds{Q}^{x,y}_{r,\epsilon}}\left[\mathscr{D}^{y}_{r,\epsilon}(0,t-\delta)\mathds{1}_{X[0,t-\delta]\subset Q^{y}_{r}}\int_{\mathds{R}^{3}}\Gamma^{r}_{\delta}(X_{t-\delta},z^{\prime})\psi^{x}_{\epsilon}(z^{\prime})dz^{\prime}\right]

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{C\mathfrak{a}^{\aleph+1}_{0}\delta(\log r)^{2\aleph+2}+\mathfrak{a}_{0}(\log r)^{2}}\mathds{E}_{\mathds{Q}^{x,y}_{r}}\left[\mathscr{D}^{y}_{r}(0,t-\delta)\mathds{1}_{X[0,t-\delta]\subset Q^{y}_{r}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{C\mathfrak{a}^{\aleph+1}_{0}\delta(\log r)^{2\aleph+2}}u_{r}^{\mathds{1},y}(t-\delta,z).

The first inequality in the above display is obtained by splitting
\mathscr{D}^{y}_{r,\epsilon}(0,t)\mathds{1}_{X[0,t]\subset Q^{y}_{r}}
as a product of
\mathscr{D}^{y}_{r,\epsilon}(0,t-\delta)\mathds{1}_{X[0,t-\delta]\subset Q^{y}_{r}}
and
\mathscr{D}^{y}_{r,\epsilon}(t-\delta,t)\mathds{1}_{X[t-\delta,t]\subset Q^{y}_{r}}
and (
5.17
) to bound
\mathscr{D}^{y}_{r,\epsilon}(t-\delta,t)\mathds{1}_{X[t-\delta,t]\subset Q^{y}_{r}}
. The second to last inequality is obtained by applying the bound on the transition kernel
\Gamma^{r}_{\delta}(X_{t-\delta},z^{\prime})
as shown in (
5.18
). From (
5.22
) and (
5.17
) again, we have for
q\in[1,\infty)

\left(\int_{Q^{y}_{r}}|u_{r}^{\mathds{1},y}(t-\delta,z)|^{q}dz\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\lesssim e^{C\mathfrak{a}^{\aleph+1}_{0}(t-\delta)(\log r)^{2\aleph+2}}.

This implies that for
q\in[1,\infty]
,

(5.24)

\displaystyle\|u^{x,y}_{r}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{q}}\leq Ce^{C\mathfrak{a}^{\aleph+1}_{0}t(\log r)^{2\aleph+2}}.

Now observe that for
\phi\in C(Q_{L})
, using Hölder inequality,

(5.25)

\displaystyle\int_{Q^{y}_{L}}u^{\phi,y}_{L}(t,x)dx=\sum_{n\in\mathds{N}}e^{t\boldsymbol{\lambda}(Q^{y}_{L})}\langle v^{y}_{n,L},\phi\rangle\langle v^{y}_{n,L},\mathds{1}_{Q^{y}_{L}}\rangle\leq e^{t\boldsymbol{\lambda}(Q^{y}_{L})}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}}\|\mathds{1}_{Q^{y}_{L}}\|_{L^{2}}.

Set
\phi=u^{x,y}_{L}(1,\cdot)
. Then by the Chapman-Kolmogorov equation, we know
u^{\mathds{1},y}_{L}(t,x)=\int_{Q^{y}_{L}}u^{\phi,y}_{L}(t-1,z)dz
. Applying (
5.22
) to the right hand side of the latter equation in conjuction with (
5.23
) and (
5.24
) yields

(5.26)

u^{\mathds{1},y}_{L}(t,x)\lesssim\exp(t\boldsymbol{\lambda}(Q^{y}_{L})+C\mathfrak{a}^{\aleph+1}_{0}(\log L)^{2\aleph+2}).

Now we proceed to prove the lower bound. Using Markov property at time
\delta\in(1,t)
and lower bound on
\mathds{1}_{X{[0,\delta]}\subset Q^{y}_{L}}\cdot\mathscr{D}^{y}_{L,\epsilon}(0,\delta)
from (
5.17
), we have

(5.27)

u^{\mathds{1},y}_{L}(t,x)\geq e^{-C\mathfrak{a}^{\aleph+1}_{0}\delta(\log L)^{2\aleph+2}}\mathds{E}_{\mathds{Q}^{x,y}_{L}}\Big[\mathds{1}_{X[0,\delta]\subset Q^{y}_{L}}u^{\mathds{1},y}_{L}(t-\delta,X_{\delta})\Big].

Then for
r\in(0,L),
we have

(5.28)

\displaystyle\mathds{E}_{\mathds{Q}^{x,y}_{L}}

\displaystyle\Big[\mathds{1}_{X[0,\delta]\subset Q^{y}_{L}}u^{\mathds{1},y}_{L}(t-\delta,X_{\delta})\Big]

\displaystyle\geq\mathds{E}_{\mathds{Q}^{x,y}_{L}}\Big[\mathds{1}_{X_{\delta}\in Q^{y}_{r}}u^{\mathds{1},y}_{r}(t-\delta,X_{\delta})\Big]-\mathds{E}_{\mathds{Q}^{x,y}_{L}}\Big[\mathds{1}_{X[0,\delta]\not\subset Q^{y}_{L}}\mathds{1}_{X_{\delta}\in Q^{y}_{r}}u^{\mathds{1},y}_{r}(t-\delta,X_{\delta})\Big].

The second term on the r.h.s. can be bounded as

\displaystyle\mathds{E}_{\mathds{Q}_{L}^{x,y}}\left[\mathds{1}_{X[0,\delta]\not\subset Q_{L}}\mathds{1}_{X_{\delta}\in Q^{y}_{r}}u^{\mathds{1},y}_{r}(t-\delta,X_{\delta})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}(X[0,\delta]\not\subset Q_{L})\sup_{z\in Q^{y}_{r}}u^{\mathds{1},y}_{r}(t-\delta,z)

\displaystyle\leq C\exp\Big(C\mathfrak{a}_{0}\delta(\log L)^{2}-\frac{L^{2}}{C\delta}+C\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{\aleph+1}(t-\delta)(\log r)^{2\aleph+2}\Big)

(5.29)

\displaystyle\leq C\exp\Big(C\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{\aleph+1}t(\log L)^{2\aleph+2}-\frac{L^{2}}{C\delta}\Big),

where we used (
5.15
) and (
5.17
) in the second inequality. Now we bound the first term in the r.h.s. of (
5.28
). Using (
5.18
), we have

(5.30)

\displaystyle\mathds{E}_{\mathds{Q}^{x,y}_{L}}\left[\mathds{1}_{X_{\delta}\in Q^{y}_{r}}u^{\mathds{1},y}_{r}(t-\delta,X_{\delta})\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{Q^{y}_{r}}\Gamma^{L}_{\delta}(x,z)u^{\mathds{1},y}_{r}(t-\delta,z)dz

\displaystyle\geq\frac{C}{t^{d/2}}e^{-C\mathfrak{a}_{0}(\log L^{2})-C\frac{\mathfrak{a}_{0}(\log L)^{2}r^{2}}{\delta}}\int_{Q^{y}_{r}}u^{\mathds{1},y}_{r}(t-\delta,z)dz.

Note that

(5.31)

u^{\mathds{1},y}_{r}(t-\delta,z)\geq C^{-1}e^{-C\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{\aleph+1}\delta(\log r)^{2\aleph+2}}u^{\delta_{x},y}_{r}(t-\delta,z)

from (
5.23
). This gives

\mathds{E}_{\mathds{Q}^{x,y}_{L}}\left[\mathds{1}_{X_{\delta}\in Q^{y}_{r}}u^{\mathds{1},y}_{r}(t-\delta,X_{\delta})\right]\geq\frac{C}{t^{d/2}}e^{-C\mathfrak{a}_{0}(\log L^{2})-C\frac{\mathfrak{a}_{0}(\log L)^{2}r^{2}}{\delta}-C\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{\aleph+1}\delta(\log r)^{2\aleph+2}}\int_{Q^{y}_{r}}u^{\delta_{x},y}_{r}(t-\delta,z)dz

We pose to observe that using the spectral decomposition of the solution (Lemma
2.1
).

u^{\delta_{z},y}_{L}(t,x)=\sum_{n\in\mathds{N}}e^{t\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n}(Q^{y}_{L})}v^{y}_{n,L}(z)v^{y}_{n,L}(x),\quad\text{for }x,z\in Q^{y}_{L}.

Using this, we can derive

\int_{Q^{y}_{L}}u^{\delta_{z},y}_{L}(t,z)dz\geq e^{t\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{n}(Q^{y}_{L})}.

Combining these with the above lower bound, we obtain

(5.32)

\mathds{E}_{\mathds{Q}^{x,y}_{L}}\left[\mathds{1}_{X_{\delta}\in Q^{y}_{r}}u^{\mathds{1},y}_{r}(t-\delta,X_{\delta})\right]\geq\frac{C}{t^{d/2}}\exp\Big(-C\mathfrak{a}^{\aleph+1}_{0}(\log L)^{2\aleph+2})-\frac{C\mathfrak{a}_{0}(\log L)^{2}r^{2}}{\delta}+(t-\delta)\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{r})\Big).

Putting (
5.2
) and (
5.32
) together, we have (
5.20
). This completes the proof.
∎

The following lemma says that the solution
u
can be represented as the sum of the localized solution on
A^{y}_{k}
where
A^{y}_{k}:=\left\{X[0,t]\subset(Q^{y}_{L_{t}^{k+1}}\setminus Q^{y}_{L_{t}^{k}})\right\}
. This helps us to employ Proposition
5.5
for the solution
u
on
\mathds{R}^{d}
.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
L_{t}:=\lfloor t^{b}\rfloor
for
b>1
. With probability one, for all
x\in Q^{y}_{L_{t}}
and
y\in\mathds{R}^{d}
, we have for
\epsilon\in[0,1]
,

u^{\mathds{1},y}_{\epsilon}(t,x)=\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}_{0}}\mathcal{U}^{y}_{k,\epsilon}(t,x),

where

\mathcal{U}^{y}_{k,\epsilon}(t,x):=\mathds{E}_{\mathds{Q}^{x,y}_{L_{t}^{k+1},\epsilon}}\left[\mathscr{D}^{y}_{L_{t}^{k+1},\epsilon}(0,t)\mathds{1}_{A^{y}_{k}}\right].

Proof.

For simplicity, we prove this result for
x=y=0
. Let us denote
u^{\epsilon}_{t}:=u^{\mathds{1},0}_{\epsilon}(t,0)
. The general case follows easily from the stationarity of the solution
u
. We let
\mathcal{U}_{k,\epsilon}(t):=\mathcal{U}^{0}_{k,\epsilon}(t,0)
for
\epsilon\in[0,1]
. When
\epsilon\in(0,1]
, the lemma is proved from the classical Feynmann-Kac representation in the proof of Theorem
3.2
. To deal with the case of
\epsilon=0
, we first estimate
\mathcal{U}_{k,\epsilon}(t)
. Recall that
\mathcal{U}_{k,\epsilon}(t)
is equal to

\mathds{E}_{\mathds{Q}^{0,0}_{L^{k+1}_{t},\epsilon}}\left[\mathds{1}_{X[0,t]\not\subset Q^{0}_{L^{k}_{t}},X[0,t]\subset Q^{0}_{L^{k+1}_{t}}}\cdot\mathscr{D}^{0}_{L^{k+1}_{t},\epsilon}(0,t)\right].

Using (
5.16
) to bound the above display yields

(5.33)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{k,\epsilon}(t)

\displaystyle\leq C\exp\Big(Ct\mathfrak{a}_{\epsilon}^{\aleph+1}((k+1)\log L_{t})^{2\aleph+2}-\frac{L_{t}^{2k}}{Ct}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq C\exp\Big(Ct\mathfrak{a}_{\epsilon}^{\aleph+1}(b(k+1)\log t)^{\aleph+2}-\frac{t^{2bk}}{Ct}\Big)

where the last inequality is obtained by substituting
L_{t}=\lfloor t^{b}\rfloor
. For a small
\delta>0
, define the following event:

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\epsilon}:=\Big\{\mathfrak{a}_{\epsilon}\leq C_{b}t^{\frac{2bk-2-\delta_{0}}{\upsilon+1}}\Big\}.

Under the event
\Upsilon_{\epsilon}
, we have that for all
t\geq t_{0}
and all
k\geq 1
,

(5.34)

\mathcal{U}_{k,\epsilon}(t)\leq C\exp\Big(-\frac{t^{2bk-1}}{2C}\Big).

By the union bound of the probability to obtain that for all
K\in\mathds{N}
and for all
\epsilon\in(0,1]

(5.35)

\displaystyle\mathds{P}\Big(u^{0}_{t}-\sum^{K}_{k=0}\mathcal{U}_{k}(t)>\delta\Big)\leq

\displaystyle\mathds{P}\Big(|u^{0}_{t}-u^{\epsilon}_{t}|>\frac{\delta}{3}\Big)+\mathds{P}\Big(|u^{\epsilon}_{t}-\sum_{k=0}^{K}\mathcal{U}_{k,\epsilon}(t)|>\frac{\delta}{3}\Big)

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=0}^{K}\mathds{P}\Big(|\mathcal{U}_{k,\epsilon}(t)-\mathcal{U}_{k}(t)|>\frac{\delta}{3K}\Big)

By
[
HL18b
, Theorem 1.1]
, we know
u^{\epsilon}_{t}
converges in probability to
u_{t}
as
\epsilon\to 0
. This shows the first term in the right hand side of (
5.35
) converges to
0
as
\epsilon\to 0
.
Theorem
3.2
shows that the convergence of
u^{\epsilon}
in every box
Q_{L_{t}}
. As a result, the third term in the right hand side of the above display also converges to
0
.
Therefore, we need to show that the second term goes to zero taking
K
large. Note that
u^{\epsilon}_{t}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{U}_{k,\epsilon}(t)
by the classical Feynman-Kac representation. Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathds{P}

\displaystyle\Big(|u^{\epsilon}_{t}-\sum_{k=0}^{K}\mathcal{U}_{k,\epsilon}(t)|>\frac{\delta}{3}\Big)\leq\mathds{P}\Big(\Big\{\Big|\sum_{k=K+1}^{\infty}\mathcal{U}_{k,\epsilon}(t)\Big|>\frac{\delta}{3}\Big\}\cap\Upsilon_{\epsilon}\Big)+\mathds{P}(\neg\Upsilon_{\epsilon}).

Due to (
5.34
),
\sum_{k=K+1}^{\infty}\mathcal{U}_{k,\epsilon}(t)
is bounded above by
\exp(-t^{2b(K-1)}/C)
for all
K>1
on the event
\Upsilon_{\epsilon}
. This shows that the first term of the right hand side of the above display converges to
0
as
K
approaches to
\infty
.
We now seek to bound
\mathds{P}(\neg\Upsilon_{\epsilon})
. By Markov’s inequality, we have

\mathds{P}(\Upsilon_{\epsilon})=\mathds{P}\big(\mathfrak{a}_{\epsilon}>C_{b}t^{\frac{2bK-2-\delta_{0}}{\aleph+1}}\big)\leq\mathds{E}[e^{h_{0}\sqrt{\mathfrak{a}_{\epsilon}}}]\cdot\exp\Big(-h_{0}\sqrt{C_{b}}t^{\frac{2bK-2-\delta_{0}}{2(\aleph+1)}}\Big).

Recall that
\mathds{E}[e^{h_{0}\sqrt{\mathfrak{a}_{\epsilon}}}]
is uniformly bounded as shown in Proposition
5.2
. Letting
K\to\infty
sends the right hand side of the above display to
0
.
This shows that the middle term of the right hand side of (
5.35
) also converges to
0
as
\epsilon\to 0
and
K\to\infty
. As a result, we get
u(t,0)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{U}_{k}(t)
in probability. Since each term of the series is non-negative, the latter identity also holds in the almost sure sense. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
L_{t}:=t^{b}
for some
b\in(\frac{1}{2},1]
. With probability one, for all
y\in\mathds{R}^{d}
with
d=2,3
,

(5.36)

\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{x\in B(y,1)}\frac{\log u^{\mathds{1},y}_{L_{t}}(t,x)}{t\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{L_{t}})}=1.

Proof.

We only prove the lemma when
d=3
. The
d=2
case follows from
[
KPvZ20
, Lemma 3.6]
. By (
5.19
), we have

\sup_{x\in B(y,1)}\frac{\log u^{\mathds{1},y}_{L_{t}}(t,x)}{t\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{L_{t}})}\leq 1+\frac{C\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{\aleph+1}(\log L_{t})^{2\aleph+2}}{t\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{L_{t}})}.

By Section
2
, we know that for enough large
t>0
,
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{L_{t}})>0
almost surely. Since
\mathfrak{a}_{0}
is almost surely finite, we have the upper bound

(5.37)

\limsup_{t\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{x\in B(y,1)}\frac{\log u^{\mathds{1},y}_{L_{t}}(t,x)}{t\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{L_{t}})}\leq 1.

Let
b\in(\frac{1}{2},1],b_{1}\in(0,b),
and
b_{2}\in(2b_{1}-1,2b-1)
. For the lower bound, by (
5.20
) with
L_{t}:=t^{b},r_{t}=t^{b_{1}},
and
\delta_{t}:=t^{b_{2}}
, we have

\sup_{x\in B(y,1)}u^{\mathds{1},y}_{L_{t}}(t,x)\geq\log A_{t}+\log(1-B_{t})

where

\displaystyle A_{t}:=\text{const}\cdot\exp\Big((t-\delta_{t})\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{r_{t}})-\frac{\mathfrak{a}_{0}r^{2}(\log L_{t})^{2}}{C\delta_{t}}-C\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{\upsilon+1}\delta_{t}(\log L_{t})^{2\upsilon+2}\Big)

and

B_{t}:=\text{const}\cdot\exp\Big(-(t-\delta_{t})\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{r_{t}})+\frac{\mathfrak{a}_{0}r_{t}^{2}(\log L_{t})^{2}}{C\delta_{t}}-\frac{L_{t}^{2}}{C\delta_{t}}-C\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{\upsilon+1}\delta_{t}(\log L_{t})^{2\upsilon+2}+C\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{\upsilon+1}t(\log L_{t})^{2\upsilon+2}\Big).

Since
2b-b_{2}>1
,
b>b_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}(Q^{y}_{r_{t}})>0
for all large
t,
we have
B_{t}\rightarrow 0
. Furthermore, since
2b_{1}-b_{2}<1
and
b_{2}<2b-1\leq 1
, we have

\liminf_{t\rightarrow\infty}\log A_{t}\geq\liminf_{t\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{r_{t}})}{\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{L_{t}})}.

Note that
\lim_{L\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{L})}{(\log L)^{2}}=\chi
for some constant
\chi>0
(see
[
HL22
, Theorem 1]
). This shows that

\liminf_{t\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{x\in B(y,1)}\frac{\log u^{\mathds{1},y}_{L_{t}}(t,x)}{t\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{L_{t}})}\geq\liminf_{t\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{r_{t}})}{\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{L_{t}})}\geq\liminf_{t\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\chi(\log r_{t})^{2}}{\chi(\log L_{t})^{2}}\geq\left({\frac{b_{1}}{b}}\right)^{2}.

Letting
b_{1}\uparrow b
, we have the lower bound. This completes the proof.

∎

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
L_{t}:=t(\log t)^{2\aleph+2}
. With probability one, for all
y\in\mathds{R}^{d}
with
d=2,3
,

(5.38)

\limsup_{t\rightarrow\infty}\left|\sup_{x\in B(y,1)}\frac{\log u^{\mathds{1},y}(t,x)}{t\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{L_{t}})}-\sup_{x\in B(y,1)}\frac{\log u^{\mathds{1},y}_{L_{t}}(t,x)}{t\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{L_{t}})}\right|=0,

and

(5.39)

\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{x\in B(y,1)}\frac{\log u^{\mathds{1},y}(t,x)}{t\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{L_{t}})}=1.

Proof.

As in the previous lemma, we only prove the case when
d=3
since the case of
d=2
follows from
[
KPvZ20
, Proposition 4.5]
. Due to the fact
\lim_{L\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{L})}{(\log L)^{2}}=\chi
, it suffices to show

(5.40)

\limsup_{t\rightarrow\infty}\left|\sup_{x\in B(y,1)}\frac{\log u^{\mathds{1},y}(t,x)}{t(\log L_{t})^{2}}-\sup_{x\in B(y,1)}\frac{\log u^{\mathds{1},y}_{L_{t}}(t,x)}{t(\log L_{t})^{2}}\right|=0.

Letting
\epsilon\to 0
in Lemma
5.6
, we have
u^{\mathds{1},y}=\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}_{0}}\mathcal{U}^{y}_{k}
. By simple observation, it follows that

(5.41)

\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\left|\sup_{x\in B(y,1)}\frac{\log\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}_{0}}\mathcal{U}^{y}_{k}(t,x)}{t(\log L_{t})^{2}}-\max\left\{\sup_{x\in B(y,1)}\frac{\log\mathcal{U}^{y}_{0}(t,x)}{t(\log L_{t})^{2}},\sup_{x\in B(y,1)}\frac{\log\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{U}^{y}_{k}(t,x)}{t(\log L_{t})^{2}}\right\}\right|=0.

Since
\mathcal{U}^{y}_{0}=u^{\mathds{1},y}_{L_{t}}
, applying Lemma
5.7
yields that

(5.42)

\sup_{x\in B(y,1)}\frac{\log\mathcal{U}^{y}_{0}(t,x)}{t(\log L_{t})^{2}}=\sup_{x\in B(y,1)}\frac{\log\mathcal{U}^{y}_{0}(t,x)}{t\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{L_{t}})}\cdot\frac{\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{L_{t}})}{(\log L_{t})^{2}}\geq\frac{\chi}{2}

for all large
t.
Moreover, from (
5.33
), we have

\sup_{x\in B(y,1)}\mathcal{U}^{y}_{k}(t,x)\leq C\exp\Big(Ct((k+1)\log L_{t})^{2\aleph+2}\Big[\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{\aleph+1}-\frac{L_{t}^{2k}}{C^{2}t^{2}((k+1)\log L_{t})^{2\aleph+2}}\Big]\Big).

Since
L_{t}=t(\log t)^{2\aleph+2}
, we have for all
k\geq 1

(5.43)

\sup_{x\in B(y,1)}\mathcal{U}^{y}_{k}(t,x)\leq e^{-Ct(\log t)^{2\aleph+2}}

for all large
t
. This implies that for all large
t
,

(5.44)

\sup_{x\in B(y,1)}\log\Big(\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{U}^{y}_{k}(t,x)\Big)\leq 0.

By combining (
5.42
) and (
5.44
) with (
5.41
), we can conclude (
5.40
), which is the first part of the lemma. The second part of the lemma follows immediately from the first part and Lemma
5.7
.
∎

6.
Spatial Multifractality and Asymptotics of the PAM: Proof of Theorem
1.1

In the remaining sections, we will show the multifractality of the solution to (
1.1
). We only consider the solution with flat initial data and write
u
for
u^{\mathds{1}}
.

6.1.
Proof of the lower bound in Theorem
1.1

In this section, we prove the lower bound of the dimension in Theorem
1.1
. The following proposition is one of the key tools for proving such lower bound.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
\epsilon>0
and
\theta>0
. There exists
n_{0}>0
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
and
x_{1},...,x_{m}\in(e^{n},e^{n+1}]^{d}
satisfying
\min_{i\neq j}\|x_{i}-x_{j}\|_{\infty}>e^{n\theta},
we have

(6.1)

\mathds{P}\left(\max_{1\leq j\leq m}\frac{\log u(t,x_{j})}{(\log\|x_{j}\|_{\infty})^{\frac{2}{4-d}}}\leq\alpha t\right)\leq\exp\left(-cm(\alpha+\epsilon)^{\frac{d}{2}}n^{\frac{d}{4-d}}e^{d\log r_{n}-\mathfrak{c}_{d}(1+\epsilon)(\alpha+\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}}n}\right)+e^{-cm\log n},

where
r_{n}:=n^{\frac{1}{2}\log t}.

Proof.

Since
x_{1},\ldots x_{m}\in(e^{n},e^{n+1}]^{d}
, we have

(6.2)

\displaystyle\mathds{P}\Big(\max_{1\leq j\leq m}\frac{\log u(t,x_{j})}{(\log\|x_{j}\|_{\infty})^{\frac{2}{4-d}}}\leq\alpha t\Big)\leq\mathds{P}\Big(\max_{1\leq j\leq m}u(t,x_{j})\leq e^{\alpha t(n+1)^{\frac{2}{4-d}}}\Big).

Let
L:=L_{n}:=t^{\log n}
. By Proposition
5.6
, there exists
t_{0}>0
such that for all
t\geq t_{0}
the series
\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}_{0}}\mathcal{U}^{y}_{k}=u
for all
y\in\mathds{R}^{d}
. Moreover, since
0\leq\mathcal{U}^{y}_{0}\leq\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}_{0}}\mathcal{U}^{y}_{k}=u
, we have

(6.3)

\displaystyle\mathds{P}\left(\max_{1\leq j\leq m}\frac{\log u(t,x_{j})}{(\log\|x_{j}\|_{\infty})^{\frac{2}{4-d}}}\leq\alpha t\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}\Big(\max_{1\leq j\leq m}\mathcal{U}^{x_{j}}_{0}(t,x_{j})\leq e^{\alpha t(n+1)^{\frac{2}{4-d}}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathds{P}\Big(\mathcal{U}^{x_{j}}_{0}(t,x_{j})\leq e^{\alpha t(n+1)^{\frac{2}{4-d}}}\Big),

where the last equality follows from the independence of
\mathcal{U}^{x_{j}}_{0}(t,x_{j})
shown in Lemma
2.4
thanks to the fact that
\min_{i\neq j}\|x_{i}-x_{j}\|_{\infty}>e^{n\theta}\gg 3L_{t}=3t^{\log n}.

We also set
r:=r_{n}:=t^{\frac{1}{2}\log n},
. Let
\epsilon>0
. Observe that for any fixed
t\geq t_{0}
, there exists
n_{0}>0
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}

(6.4)

\displaystyle\epsilon tn\gg(\mathfrak{a}_{0}\log L_{n})^{2\aleph+2}+\frac{\mathfrak{a}_{0}(\log L_{n})^{2}r_{n}^{2}}{C\delta},\quad\frac{L_{n}^{2}}{C\delta}\gg t(\mathfrak{a}_{0}\log L_{n})^{2\aleph+2}+\frac{\mathfrak{a}_{0}(\log L_{n})^{2}r_{n}^{2}}{C\delta}

on the event
\Upsilon_{n}:=\{\mathfrak{a}_{0}\leq(\log n)^{2}\}.

Using this observation, when
d=3
, there exist
t_{0}>0
and
n^{\prime}_{0}>0
such that for all
n\geq n^{\prime}_{0}
and all
t\geq t_{0}

\displaystyle\mathds{P}\Big(\mathcal{U}^{x_{j}}_{0}(t,x_{j})\leq e^{\alpha t(n+1)^{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}\Big(e^{-C\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{\aleph+1}\delta(\log L_{n})^{2\aleph+2}-\frac{C\mathfrak{a}_{0}(\log L_{n})^{2}r_{n}^{2}}{\delta}+(t-\delta)\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{r_{n}})}-e^{C\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{2\aleph+1}t(\log L_{n})^{2\aleph+2}-\frac{L_{n}^{2}}{C\delta}}\leq e^{\alpha t(n+1)^{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}\Big(\Big\{\frac{1}{2}\exp\Big((t-\delta)\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{x_{j}}_{r_{n}})-C\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{\aleph+1}\delta(\log L_{n})^{2\aleph+2}-\frac{C\mathfrak{a}_{0}(\log L_{n})^{2}r^{2}}{\delta}\Big)\leq e^{\alpha t(n+1)^{2}}\Big\}\cap\Upsilon_{n}\Big)+\mathds{P}(\neg\Upsilon_{n})

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}\left(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{x_{j}}_{r_{n}})\leq(\alpha+\epsilon)n^{2}\right)+\mathds{P}(\neg\Upsilon_{n})

The first inequality in the above display is obtained by using the lower bound on
\mathcal{U}^{x_{j}}_{0}(t,x_{j})
from Proposition
5.5
. While the second inequality is just consequence of the union bound, the last inequality utilizes (
6.4
). When
d=2
, we use the similar argument except the fact that the lower bound on
\mathcal{U}_{0}^{x_{j}}(t,x_{j})
is now provided by Lemma 5.2 of
[
KPvZ20
]
. We obtain similarly

\displaystyle\mathds{P}(\mathcal{U}_{0}^{x_{j}}(t,x_{j})\leq e^{\alpha t(n+1)})

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}\Big(\exp\Big((t-\delta)\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{x^{j}}_{r_{n}})-\frac{r_{n}^{2}}{C\delta}-C\delta(\mathfrak{a}_{0}\log L_{n})^{5})-\exp(Ct(\mathfrak{a}_{0}\log L_{n})^{5}-\frac{L_{n}^{2}}{C\delta}\Big)\leq e^{\alpha t(n+1)}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}\Big(\Big\{\frac{1}{2}\exp\Big((t-\delta)\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{x^{j}}_{r_{n}})-\frac{r_{n}^{2}}{C\delta}-C\delta(\mathfrak{a}_{0}\log L_{n})^{5}\Big)\leq e^{\alpha t(n+1)}\Big\}\cap\Upsilon_{n}\Big)+\mathds{P}(\neg\Upsilon_{n})

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{x^{j}}_{r_{n}})\leq(\alpha+\epsilon)n)+\mathds{P}(\neg\Upsilon_{n})

Here we note that
t_{0}
can be chosen independently of
\alpha
. Now we use Lemma
2.2
to obtain

\max_{1\leq j\leq m}\mathds{P}\left(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{x_{j}}_{r_{n}})\leq(\alpha+\epsilon)n^{\frac{2}{4-d}}\right)\leq\exp\left(-c_{2}(\alpha+\epsilon)^{\frac{d}{2}}n^{\frac{d}{4-d}}e^{d\log r_{n}-\mathfrak{c}_{d}(1+\epsilon)(\alpha+\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}}n}\right)

Substituting this into (
6.3
) and (
6.2
), we have

\displaystyle\mathds{P}

\displaystyle\left(\max_{1\leq j\leq m}\frac{\log u(t,x_{j})}{(\log\|x_{j}\|_{\infty})^{\frac{2}{4-d}}}\leq\alpha t\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{m-1}\exp\left(-c_{2}m(\alpha+\epsilon)^{\frac{d}{2}}n^{\frac{d}{4-d}}e^{d\log r_{n}-\mathfrak{c}_{d}(1+\epsilon)(\alpha+\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}}n}\right)+2^{m-1}\mathds{P}(\neg\Upsilon_{n})^{m}

On the other hand, since
\mathds{E}[e^{h_{0}\sqrt{\mathfrak{a}_{0}}}]<\infty
(see
5.4
), we use Markov’s inequality to get
\mathds{P}(\neg\Upsilon_{n})\leq e^{-h_{0}\log n}
. This shows that

\mathds{P}\left(\max_{1\leq j\leq m}\frac{\log u(t,x_{j})}{(\log\|x_{j}\|_{\infty})^{\frac{2}{4-d}}}\leq\alpha t\right)\leq\exp\left(-cm(\alpha+\epsilon)^{\frac{d}{2}}n^{\frac{d}{4-d}}e^{d\log r_{n}-\mathfrak{c}_{d}(1+\epsilon)(\alpha+\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}}n}\right)+e^{-cm\log n}

for some constant
c>0
and hence, completes the proof.
∎

Now we proceed to prove Theorem
1.1
. Recall that

\mathcal{P}^{d}_{t}(\alpha)=\left\{x\in\mathds{R}^{d}\,:\,u(t,x)\geq e^{\alpha t(\log|x|)^{\frac{2}{4-d}}}\right\}.

The following result proves a lower bound to the macroscopic Hausdorff dimension of the set
\mathcal{P}^{d}_{t}(\alpha)
. The proof of the upper bound is deferred to the subsection after the following result.

Theorem 6.2
.

There exists a non-random finite constant
t_{0}>0
such that for all
t\geq t_{0}
,

\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}[\mathcal{P}^{d}_{t}(\alpha)]\geq d-\alpha^{\frac{4-d}{2}}\mathfrak{c}_{d},\quad\text{a.s.}

Proof.

Let us choose
\alpha>0
satisfying
\alpha^{\frac{4-d}{2}}\mathfrak{c}_{d}<d
. Fix
\epsilon>0
such that
\mathfrak{c}_{d}(1+\epsilon)(\alpha+\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}}<d
. Define

\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{d}_{t}(\alpha):=\mathcal{P}^{d}_{t}(\alpha)\cap\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(e^{n},e^{n+1}\right]^{d}.

Then it suffices to show that

\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}[\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{d}_{t}(\alpha)]\geq d-\alpha^{\frac{4-d}{2}}\mathfrak{c}_{d},

with probability one. We now choose
\gamma\in(\mathfrak{c}_{d}(1+\epsilon)(\alpha+\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}}/d,1)
and define, for all integers
n\geq 0
,

a_{j,n}(\gamma):=e^{n}+je^{n\gamma},\quad j\in[0,e^{n(1-\gamma)})\cap\mathds{Z},

and

I_{n}(\gamma):=\bigcup_{j\in[0,e^{n(1-\gamma)})\cap\mathds{Z}}\{a_{j,n}(\gamma)\},\qquad\mathcal{I}_{n}(\gamma):=\prod_{k=1}^{d}I^{k}_{n}(\gamma),

where
I^{k}_{n}(\gamma)
is a copy of
I_{n}(\gamma)
for all
1\leq j\leq d
. We choose
x\in\mathcal{I}_{n}(\gamma)
and
\theta\in(0,\gamma-\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{d}(1+\epsilon)(\alpha+\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}}}{d})
. By the construction of the set
\mathcal{I}_{n}(\gamma)
, we first find the points
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m(n)}
satisfying the followings:
(a.1)
x_{i}\in\mathcal{I}_{n}(\gamma)\cap B(x,e^{n\gamma})
for all
i=1,...,m(n)
;
(a.2)
\|x_{i}-x_{j}\|_{\infty}\geq e^{n\theta}
whenever
1\leq i<j\leq m(n)
; (a.3)
d^{-1}e^{dn(\gamma-\theta)}\leq m(n)\leq de^{dn(\gamma-\theta)}
.
Then by Proposition
6.1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathds{P}

\displaystyle\left(\max_{1\leq j\leq m(n)}\frac{\log u(t,x_{j})}{(\log\|x_{j}\|_{\infty})^{\frac{2}{4-d}}}\leq\alpha t\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-cm(n)(\alpha+\epsilon)^{\frac{d}{2}}n^{\frac{d}{4-d}}e^{d\log r_{n}-\mathfrak{c}_{d}(1+\epsilon)(\alpha+\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}}n}\right)+e^{-cm(n)\log n}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{c}{2}(\alpha+\epsilon)^{\frac{d}{2}}n^{\frac{d}{4-d}}e^{c\log n+[d(\gamma-\theta)-\mathfrak{c}_{d}(1+\epsilon)(\alpha+\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}}]n}\right)+e^{-\frac{c}{2}e^{dn(\gamma-\theta)}\log n}.

By our choice of
\gamma
and
\theta
,
\kappa:=d(\gamma-\theta)-\mathfrak{c}_{d}(1+\epsilon)(\alpha+\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}}>0
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathds{P}\left(\max_{\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m(n)}\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{n}(\theta)\cap B(x,e^{n\gamma})}\frac{\log u(t,x_{i})}{(\log\|x_{i}\|_{\infty})^{2}}\leq\alpha t\right)\leq\exp\left(-C_{1}e^{\kappa n-C_{2}\log n}\right)+e^{-\frac{c}{2}e^{dn(\gamma-\theta)}\log n},

for some constant
C_{1},C_{2}>0
. Then we have

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathds{P}

\displaystyle\left(\min_{x\in\mathcal{I}_{n}(\gamma)}\max_{\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m(n)}\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{n}(\theta)\cap B(x,e^{n\gamma})}\frac{\log u(t,x_{i})}{(\log\|x_{i}\|_{\infty})^{2}}\leq\alpha t\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{I}_{n}(\gamma)}\mathds{P}\left(\max_{1\leq i\leq m(n)}\frac{\log u(t,x_{i})}{(\log\|x_{i}\|_{\infty})^{2}}\leq\alpha t\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}Ce^{2n(1-\gamma)}\left(\exp\left(-C_{1}e^{\kappa n-C_{2}\log n}\right)+e^{-\frac{c}{2}e^{3n(\gamma-\theta)}\log n}\right)<\infty.

for some constant
C>0
. Hence, the Borel-Cantelli lemma implies that
\mathcal{P}^{d}
contains a
\gamma-
thick set (see Definition
B.2
) almost surely. By Proposition
B.3
, We get
\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{d}_{t}(\alpha))\geq d(1-\gamma)
with probability one. Letting
\gamma\downarrow\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{d}(1+\epsilon)(\alpha+\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}}}{d}
and
\theta\downarrow 0
with
0<\theta<\gamma-\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{d}(1+\epsilon)(\alpha+\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}}}{d}
, we get
\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{d}_{t}(\alpha))\geq d-\mathfrak{c}_{d}(1+\epsilon)(\alpha+\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}}.
Since
\epsilon>0
is arbitrary, by the monotonicity of the macroscopic Hausdorff dimension, we conclude that

\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}[\mathcal{P}^{d}_{t}(\alpha)]\geq\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}[\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{d}_{t}(\alpha)]\geq d-\alpha^{\frac{4-d}{2}}\mathfrak{c}_{d},

almost surely.

∎

6.2.
Proof of the upper bound in Theorem
1.1

In this section, we prove the upper bound in Theorem
1.1
. The following proposition will be used to complete the proof.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
\epsilon>0
and
M>0
. There exist
b:=b(M)>1
and
n_{0}:=n_{0}(M,b,\epsilon)>0
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
and
t\geq 1

(6.5)

\mathds{P}\Big(\sup_{x\in\mathcal{B}(y,1)}\frac{\log u(t,x)}{(\log\|x\|_{\infty})^{\frac{2}{4-d}}}\geq\alpha t,\mathfrak{a}_{0}\leq M\Big)\leq c_{1}(\alpha-\epsilon)^{\frac{d}{2}}n^{\frac{d}{4-d}}e^{d\log L_{t}-(1-\epsilon)\mathfrak{c}_{d}(\alpha-\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}}n},

where
L_{t}:=t^{b}
and
y\in\mathds{S}_{n}
for
n\in\mathds{N}
.

Proof.

Since we can write
u(t,x)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{y}(t,x)
due to Lemma
5.6
, we have

\mathds{P}\Big(\sup_{x\in\mathcal{B}(y,1)}\frac{\log u(t,x)}{(\log\|x\|_{\infty})^{\frac{2}{4-d}}}\geq\alpha t,\mathfrak{a}_{0}\leq M\Big)\leq\mathds{P}\Big(\sup_{x\in B(y,1)}u(t,x)\geq\frac{1}{2}e^{\alpha tn^{\frac{2}{4-d}}},\mathfrak{a}_{0}\leq M\Big)\leq({\bf C_{1}})+({\bf C_{1}}),

where

\displaystyle({\bf C_{1}})

\displaystyle:=\mathds{P}\Big(\sup_{x\in B(y,1)}\mathcal{U}_{0}^{y}(t,x)\geq\frac{1}{2}e^{\alpha tn^{\frac{2}{4-d}}},\mathfrak{a}_{0}\leq M\Big),

\displaystyle({\bf C_{2}})

\displaystyle:=\mathds{P}\Big(\sup_{x\in B(y,1)}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{y}(t,x)\geq\frac{1}{2}e^{\alpha tn^{\frac{2}{4-d}}},\mathfrak{a}_{0}\leq M\Big)

We first bound
({\bf C_{2}})
. When
d=3
, using (
5.33
), on the event
\{\mathfrak{a}_{0}\leq M\}
we have

\mathcal{U}^{y}_{k}(t,x)\leq C\exp\left(CtM^{\aleph+1}(b(k+1)\log t)^{2\aleph+2}-\frac{t^{2bk}}{Ct}\right).

Therefore, we can choose a large
b:=b(M)>0
such that for all
t\geq e
and
n\geq 1
,

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{y}(t,x)\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}C\exp\left(-C_{1}t^{2bk-1}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}e^{\alpha tn^{\frac{2}{4-d}}}.

for some constant
C_{1}>0
. This implies that
({\bf C_{2}})=0
for all
t\geq e
. For
d=2
case, one can use Lemma 5.2 of
[
KPvZ20
]
to get the same result.

Now we proceed to bound
({\bf C_{1}})
. Applying Proposition
5.5
for
d=3
, there exists
n_{0}=n_{0}(M,b,\epsilon)>0
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}

\displaystyle({\bf C_{1}})

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}\Big(C\exp(t\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{L_{t}})+CM^{\upsilon+1}(\log L_{t})^{2\upsilon+2})\geq\frac{1}{2}e^{\alpha tn^{\frac{2}{4-d}}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}\Big(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{L_{t}})\geq\alpha n^{\frac{2}{4-d}}-\frac{\log(2C)+CM^{\upsilon+1}(b\log t)^{2\upsilon+2}}{t}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{L_{t}})\geq(\alpha-\epsilon)n^{\frac{2}{4-d}}),

The
d=2
case follows similarly from using Lemma 5.2 of
[
KPvZ20
]
. By Lemma
2.2
, we further have

({\bf C_{1}})\leq\mathds{P}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{L_{t}})\geq(\alpha-\epsilon)n^{\frac{2}{4-d}})\leq c_{1}(\alpha-\epsilon)^{\frac{d}{2}}n^{\frac{d}{4-d}}e^{db\log t-(1-\epsilon)\mathfrak{c}_{d}(\alpha-\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}}n}.

Summing the bounds
({\bf C_{1}})
and
({\bf C_{2}})
, we arrive at

({\bf C_{1}})+({\bf C_{2}})\leq c_{1}(\alpha-\epsilon)^{\frac{d}{2}}n^{\frac{d}{4-d}}e^{db\log t-(1-\epsilon)\mathfrak{c}_{d}(\alpha-\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}}n},

which completes the proof.
∎

Theorem 6.4
.

For all
t\geq e
and all
\alpha\in(0,\left(d/\mathfrak{c}_{d}\right)^{\frac{2}{4-d}})
,

(6.6)

\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}[\mathcal{P}^{d}_{t}(\alpha)]=\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}\Big[\Big\{x\in\mathds{R}^{d}\,:\,u(t,x)\geq e^{\alpha t(\log\|x\|_{\infty})^{\frac{2}{4-d}}}\Big\}\Big]\leq d-\alpha^{\frac{4-d}{2}}\mathfrak{c}_{d},

with probability one.

Proof.

Fix
M>0
. By Markov’s inequality and Proposition
5.2
, we have

(6.7)

\mathds{P}(\mathfrak{a}_{0}\geq M)\leq\delta_{M}:=\mathds{E}[e^{h_{0}\sqrt{\mathfrak{a}_{0}}}]\cdot e^{-h_{0}\sqrt{M}}.

Note that
\delta_{M}\rightarrow 0
as
M\rightarrow\infty
. Choose
\epsilon\in(0,\alpha\wedge 1)
and
\rho\in(d-(1-\epsilon)\mathfrak{c}_{d}(\alpha-\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}},d)
. Using Proposition
6.3
, we have that for all
t\geq 1

(6.8)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}

\displaystyle e^{-n\rho}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in\mathds{N}^{d}\\
B(y,1)\subset\mathds{S}_{n}\end{subarray}}\mathds{P}\Big(\sup_{x\in B(y,1)}\frac{\log u(t,x)}{(\log\|x\|_{\infty})^{\frac{2}{4-d}}}\geq\alpha t,\mathfrak{a}_{0}\leq M\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}c_{1}(\alpha-\epsilon)^{\frac{d}{2}}n^{\frac{d}{4-d}}\exp\Big(db\log t+[d-\rho-\mathfrak{c}_{d}\alpha_{\epsilon}^{\frac{4-d}{2}}]n\Big)<\infty,

where
b=b(M)>0
is taken as in Proposition
6.3
and
\alpha_{\epsilon}:=(1-\epsilon)^{\frac{2}{4-d}}(\alpha-\epsilon)
. Recall the definition of Hausdorff content
\nu^{n}_{\rho}(E)
of any set
E
from Section
B.1
. The first line in (
6.8
) is an upper bound to
\mathbb{E}[\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\nu^{n}_{\rho}(\mathcal{P}^{d}_{t}(\alpha))\mathbbm{1}(\mathfrak{a}_{0}\leq M)]
. As a result, we have

\mathds{P}\Big(\{\mathfrak{a}_{0}\leq M\}\cap\Big\{\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\nu^{n}_{\rho}(\mathcal{P}^{d}_{t}(\alpha))<\infty\Big\}\Big)=\mathds{P}(\{\mathfrak{a}_{0}\leq M\}).

Combining (
6.7
) with the above display yields

\mathds{P}\Big(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\nu^{n}_{\rho}(\mathcal{P}^{d}_{t}(\alpha))<\infty\Big)\geq 1-\mathds{E}[e^{h_{0}\sqrt{\mathfrak{a}_{0}}}]\cdot e^{-h_{0}\sqrt{M}}.

Note that the l.h.s. of the above inequality does not depend on
M
. Thus, by letting
M\rightarrow\infty
, we have
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\nu^{n}_{\rho}(\mathcal{P}^{d}_{t}(\alpha))<\infty
a.s., which implies that
\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}[\mathcal{P}^{d}_{t}(\alpha)]\leq\rho
for any
\rho\in(d-\mathfrak{c}_{d}\alpha_{\epsilon}^{\frac{4-d}{2}},d)
, a.s. By taking
\rho\downarrow d-\mathfrak{c}_{d}\alpha_{\epsilon}^{\frac{4-d}{2}}
and
\epsilon\downarrow 0
, we get
\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}[\mathcal{P}^{d}_{t}(\alpha)]\leq d-\mathfrak{c}_{d}\alpha^{\frac{4-d}{2}}.

∎

6.3.
Spatial asymptotics of the PAM

This section is devoted to proving the spatial asymptotics (
1.5
). This result is a consequence of the first part of Theorem
1.1
, i.e., there exists
t_{0}>0
such that

(6.9)

\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}[\mathcal{P}^{d}_{t}(\alpha)]=\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}\left(\left\{x\in\mathds{R}^{d}\,:\,\frac{\log u(t,x)}{\log|x|}\geq\alpha t\right\}\right)=(d-\alpha^{\frac{4-d}{2}}\mathfrak{c}_{d})\vee 0,\quad\text{a.s.,}

for
t\geq t_{0}
and
\alpha>0
. We provide the details of the proof below.

Proof of (
1.5
).

By the definition of the macroscopic Hausdorff dimension,
\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}(A)>0,A\subset\mathds{R}^{d}
implies that
A
is an unbounded set. This fact and (
6.9
) imply that for any
\alpha<(d/\mathfrak{c}_{d})^{\frac{2}{4-d}}
, the set
\mathcal{P}^{d}_{t}(\alpha)
is unbounded, hence in particular

(6.10)

\limsup_{\|x\|_{\infty}\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log u(t,x)}{(\log\|x\|_{\infty})^{\frac{2}{4-d}}}\geq\alpha t,\quad\text{a.s.}

This implies that

(6.11)

\limsup_{\|x\|_{\infty}\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log u(t,x)}{(\log\|x\|_{\infty})^{\frac{2}{4-d}}}\geq\left(\frac{d}{\mathfrak{c}_{d}}\right)^{\frac{2}{4-d}}t,\quad\text{a.s.}

Now we prove the upper bound. Fix
\epsilon\in(0,\alpha\wedge 1)
. Let
M>0
and
\delta_{M}
be defined as in (
6.7
). Note that we can rewrite (
6.8
) as

(6.12)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in\mathds{N}^{d}\\
B(y,1)\subset\mathds{S}_{n}\end{subarray}}\mathds{P}\Big(\sup_{x\in B(y,1)}\frac{\log u(t,x)}{(\log\|x\|_{\infty})^{\frac{2}{4-d}}}\geq g(d,\epsilon)t,\mathfrak{a}_{0}\leq M\Big)<\infty,

where

g(d,\epsilon):=\big(d/\mathfrak{c}_{d}(1-\epsilon)\big)^{\frac{2}{4-d}}+\epsilon.

The Borel-Cantelli lemma yields that with probability greater than
1-e^{-h\sqrt{M}}\mathbb{E}[e^{h\sqrt{\mathfrak{a}_{0}}}]
,

\limsup_{\|x\|_{\infty}\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log u(t,x)}{(\log\|x\|_{\infty})^{\frac{2}{4-d}}}\leq\left(\frac{d}{\mathfrak{c}_{d}(1-\epsilon)}+\epsilon\right)^{\frac{2}{4-d}}t,

for all
t\geq t_{0}.
Letting
\epsilon\to 0
and
M\to\infty
, we can conclude that for all
t\geq t_{0}

\limsup_{\|x\|_{\infty}\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log u(t,x)}{(\log\|x\|_{\infty})^{\frac{2}{4-d}}}\leq\left(\frac{d}{\mathfrak{c}_{d}}\right)^{\frac{2}{4-d}}t

with probability one. This completes the proof.
∎

7.
Spatio-temporal Multifractality: Proof of Theorem
1.2

7.1.
Proof of the lower bound in Theorem
1.2

Proposition 7.1
.

Let
\epsilon>0
and
\theta>0
. There exists
c,t_{0}>0
such that for all
t\geq t_{0}
and
x_{1},...,x_{m}\in\mathds{R}^{d}
satisfying
\min_{i\neq j}\|x_{i}-x_{j}\|_{\infty}>3L_{t}
where
L_{t}:=t
, we have

(7.1)

\mathds{P}\left(\max_{1\leq j\leq m}\log u(t,x_{j})\leq\beta t^{\frac{6-d}{4-d}}\right)\leq\exp\left(-cm(\beta+\epsilon)^{\frac{d}{2}}t^{\frac{d}{4-d}}e^{d\log r_{t}-\mathfrak{c}_{d}(1+\epsilon)(\beta+\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}}t}\right)+e^{-cm\log t},

where
r_{t}:=t^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of Proposition
6.1
. By Lemma
5.6
, we have

\displaystyle\mathds{P}\left(\max_{1\leq j\leq m}\log u(t,x_{j})\leq\beta t^{\frac{6-d}{4-d}}\right)\leq\mathds{P}\Big(\max_{1\leq j\leq m}\mathcal{U}^{x_{j}}_{0}(t,x_{j})\leq e^{\beta t^{\frac{6-d}{4-d}}}\Big)=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathds{P}\Big(\mathcal{U}^{x_{j}}_{0}(t,x_{j})\leq e^{\beta t^{\frac{6-d}{4-d}}}\Big),

whenever
\min_{i\neq j}\|x_{i}-x_{j}\|_{\infty}>3L_{t}
where
L_{t}:=t
. Let
r:=r_{t}:=t^{1/2}
. The last equality follows due to the independence between
\{\mathcal{U}^{x_{j}}_{0}(t,x_{j})\}_{1\leq j\leq m}
. There exist
t_{0}>0
such that for all
t\geq t_{0}

\epsilon t^{2}\gg(\mathfrak{a}_{0}\log L_{t})^{2\aleph+2}+\frac{\mathfrak{a}_{0}(\log L_{t})^{2}r_{t}^{2}}{C\delta},\quad\frac{L_{t}^{2}}{C\delta}\gg t(\mathfrak{a}_{0}\log L_{t})^{2\aleph+2}+\frac{\mathfrak{a}_{0}(\log L_{t})^{2}r_{t}^{2}}{C\delta}

on the event

\Upsilon_{t}:=\{\mathfrak{a}_{0}\leq(\log t)^{2}\}.

For
d=3
, by Proposition
5.5
there exists
t_{0}>0
such that for all
t\geq t_{0}
,

\displaystyle\mathds{P}

\displaystyle\Big(\mathcal{U}^{x_{j}}_{0}(t,x_{j})\leq e^{\beta t^{3}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}\Big(e^{-C\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{\aleph+1}\delta(\log L_{t})^{2\aleph+2}-\frac{C\mathfrak{a}_{0}(\log L_{t})^{2}r_{t}^{2}}{\delta}+(t-\delta)\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{r_{t}})}-e^{C\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{2\aleph+1}t(\log L_{t})^{2\aleph+2}-\frac{L_{t}^{2}}{C\delta}}\leq e^{\beta t^{3}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}\Big(\Big\{\frac{1}{2}\exp\Big((t-\delta)\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{x_{j}}_{r_{n}})-C\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{\aleph+1}\delta(\log L)^{2\aleph+2}-\frac{C\mathfrak{a}_{0}(\log L)^{2}r^{2}}{\delta}\Big)\leq e^{\beta t^{3}}\Big\}\cap\Upsilon_{t}\Big)+\mathds{P}(\neg\Upsilon_{t})

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}\left(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{x_{j}}_{r_{t}})\leq(\beta+\epsilon)t^{2}\right)+\mathds{P}(\neg\Upsilon_{t}).

For
d=2
, we can proceed similarly using Lemma 5.2 of
[
KPvZ20
]
to obtain

\displaystyle\mathds{P}(\mathcal{U}_{0}^{x_{j}}(t,x_{j})\leq e^{\beta t^{2}})

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}\Big(\exp\Big((t-\delta)\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{x^{j}}_{r_{t}})-\frac{r_{t}^{2}}{C\delta}-C\delta(\mathfrak{a}_{0}\log L_{t})^{5})-\exp(Ct(\mathfrak{a}_{0}\log L_{t})^{5}-\frac{L_{t}^{2}}{C\delta}\Big)\leq e^{\beta t^{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}\Big(\Big\{\frac{1}{2}\exp((t-\delta)\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{x^{j}}_{r_{t}})-\frac{r_{t}^{2}}{C\delta}-C\delta(\mathfrak{a}_{0}\log L_{t})^{5})\leq e^{\beta t^{2}}\Big\}\cap\Upsilon_{t}\Big)+\mathds{P}(\neg\Upsilon_{n})

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{x^{j}}_{r_{t}})\leq(\beta+\epsilon)t)+\mathds{P}(\neg\Upsilon_{t})

By Lemma
2.2
, we have

\max_{1\leq j\leq m}\mathds{P}\left(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{x_{j}}_{r_{t}})\leq(\beta+\epsilon)t^{\frac{6-d}{4-d}}\right)\leq\exp(-c_{2}(\beta+\epsilon)^{\frac{d}{2}}t^{\frac{d}{4-d}}e^{d\log r_{t}-\mathfrak{c}_{d}(1+\epsilon)(\beta+\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}}t}).

Moreover, we have
\mathds{P}(\neg\Upsilon_{t})\leq e^{-h_{0}\log t}
for some
h_{0}>0
by the fact that
\mathds{E}[e^{h_{0}\sqrt{\mathfrak{a}_{0}}}]<\infty
. This yields that

\mathds{P}\left(\max_{1\leq j\leq m}\log u(t,x_{j})\leq\beta t^{3}\right)\leq 2^{m-1}\exp(-cm(\beta+\epsilon)^{\frac{d}{2}}t^{\frac{d}{4-d}}e^{d\log r_{t}-\mathfrak{c}_{d}(1+\epsilon)(\beta+\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}}t})+2^{m-1}e^{-h_{0}m\log t}.

By choosing
t_{0}
large such that
2^{m-1}e^{-h_{0}m\log t}<e^{-cm\log t}
for some constant
c
, we achieve the bound in (
7.1
). This completes the proof.
∎

Now we are ready to prove the lower bound in Theorem
1.2
. Recall that

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{d}(\beta,v)=\left\{(t,x)\in(1,\infty)\,:\,u(v\log t,x)>e^{\beta(v\log t)^{\frac{2}{4-d}}}\right\}

Theorem 7.2
.

For every
\beta,v>0
, with probability one,

(7.2)

\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}[\mathcal{P}^{d}(\beta,v)]\geq(d+1-\beta^{\frac{4-d}{2}}v\mathfrak{c})\vee d.

Proof.

Choose
\beta,\epsilon,v>0
such that
(\beta+\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}}(1+\epsilon)v\mathfrak{c}_{d}<d+1
.
Let us define

\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{d}(\beta,v):=\mathcal{P}^{d}(\beta,v)\cap\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(e^{n},e^{n+1}\right]^{d+1}.

Since
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{d}(\beta,v)\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{d}(\beta,v)
, it is enough to show
\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}[\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{d}(\beta,v)]\geq(d+1-\beta^{\frac{4-d}{2}}v\mathfrak{c}_{d})\vee d
with probability one. We choose
\gamma\in(\frac{(\beta+\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}}(1+\epsilon)v\mathfrak{c}_{d}}{d},1)
and
\theta\in(0,\gamma-\frac{(\beta+\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}}(1+\epsilon)v\mathfrak{c}_{d}}{d})
.
We borrow the notations
a_{j,n}
and
I_{n}(\gamma)

from Theorem
6.2
. Suppose
I^{k}_{n}(\gamma)
are copies of
I_{k}(n)
for
1\leq k\leq d+1
. We introduce further

\tilde{\mathcal{I}}_{n}(\gamma):=\prod_{k=1}^{d+1}I^{k}_{n}(\gamma),

where
I^{k}_{n}(\gamma)
is a copy of
I_{n}(\gamma)
for all
1\leq j\leq d+1
. We choose
x\in\tilde{\mathcal{I}}_{n}(\gamma)
and take the points
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m(n)}
such that they satisfy the following conditions:
(b.1)

x_{i}\in\tilde{\mathcal{I}}_{n}(\gamma)\cap B(x,e^{n\gamma})
for all
i=1,...,m(n)
;
(b.2)

|x_{i}-x_{j}|\geq e^{n\theta}
whenever
1\leq i<j\leq m(n)
;
(b.3)

d^{-1}e^{dn(\gamma-\theta)}\leq m(n)\leq de^{dn(\gamma-\theta)}
.

Observe that
e^{n\theta}\gg 3L_{v\log t}=3(v\log t)
for all
t\in(e^{n},e^{n+1}]
. We now notice that there exists
n_{0}>0
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
,

\displaystyle\mathds{P}

\displaystyle\Big(\mathcal{P}^{d}_{1}\cap(\{t\}\times B(x,e^{n\gamma}))=\varnothing\text{ for some }t\in(e^{n},e^{n+1}]\text{ and }x\in\tilde{\mathcal{I}}_{n}(\gamma)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}\Big(\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\cap\mathds{Z}\\
t\in(e^{n},e^{n+1}]\end{subarray}}\min_{x\in\tilde{\mathcal{I}}_{n}(\gamma)}\max_{\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m(n)}\subseteq\tilde{\mathcal{I}}_{n}(\theta)\cap B(x,e^{n\gamma})}\Big\{e^{-\beta(v\log t)^{\frac{6-d}{4-d}}}u(v\log t,x)\Big\}\leq 1\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\cap\mathds{Z}\\
t\in(e^{n},e^{n+1}]\end{subarray}}\sum_{x\in\tilde{\mathcal{I}}_{n}(\gamma)}\mathds{P}\left(\max_{\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m(n)}\subseteq\tilde{\mathcal{I}}_{n}(\theta)\cap B(x,e^{n\gamma})}u(v\log t,x)\leq e^{\beta(v\log t)^{\frac{6-d}{4-d}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{dn(1-\gamma)+n}\cdot\exp\left(-cm(n)(\beta+\epsilon)^{\frac{d}{2}}(v(n+1))^{\frac{d}{4-d}}e^{d\log r_{v(n+1)}-\mathfrak{c}_{d}(1+\epsilon)(\beta+\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}}v(n+1)}\right)+e^{-cm(n)v(n+1)}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{dn(1-\gamma)+n}\cdot\Bigg[\exp\left(-c(\beta+\epsilon)^{\frac{d}{2}}v(n+1)^{\frac{d}{4-d}}e^{\log[v(n+1)]+\kappa n-\mathfrak{c}_{d}(1+\epsilon)(\beta+\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}}v}\right)

\displaystyle+\exp\left(-\frac{c}{2}e^{dn(\gamma-\theta)}v(n+1)\right)\Bigg],

where
\kappa:=d(\gamma-\theta)-\mathfrak{c}_{d}(1+\epsilon)(\beta+\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}}v>0
by the choice of
\gamma
and
\theta
. The first inequality is straightforward. The inequality follows by applying the union bound and the third inequality is obtained by applying Proposition
7.1
. The right hand side of the above inequality is summable w.r.t.
n
. Hence, by the Borel-Cantelli lemma, there exists
n_{0}>0
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}

(7.3)

\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{d}(\beta,v)\cap(\{t\}\times B(x,e^{n\gamma}))\neq\varnothing\text{ for all }t\in(e^{n},e^{n+1}]\text{ and all }x\in\mathcal{I}_{n}(\gamma).

This implies that
\mu_{n}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{d}(\beta,v))\geq Ce^{dn(1-\gamma)+n}
where
\mu_{n}
is defined in Proposition
B.4
. Therefore, by Proposition
B.4
we can deduce that
\sum_{n}\nu_{n,d+1-d\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{d}(\beta,v))=\infty
almost surely, which shows that
\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{d}(\beta,v))\geq 4-3\gamma
. Letting
\gamma\downarrow\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{d}(1+\epsilon)(\beta+\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}}v}{d}
and
\theta\downarrow 0
without violating
\theta\in(0,\gamma-\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{d}(1+\epsilon)(\beta+\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}}v}{d})
, we get
\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{d}(\beta,v))\geq d+1-\mathfrak{c}_{d}(1+\epsilon)(\beta+\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}}v
. Since
\epsilon>0
is arbitrary,
\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{d}(\beta,v))\geq d+1-\beta^{\frac{4-d}{2}}v\mathfrak{c}_{d}
.

Now it remains to show
\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{d}(\beta,v))\geq d
, a.s. for any
\beta,v>0
. First note that

\left\{(s,x)\in(1,\infty)\times\mathds{R}^{d}\,:\,u(v\log s,x)>e^{\beta(v\log s)^{\frac{6-d}{4-d}}}\right\}\supseteq\{t\}\times\left\{x\in\mathds{R}^{d}\,:\,u(v\log t,x)>e^{\beta(v\log t)^{\frac{6-d}{4-d}}}\right\},

for all
t\geq 1
. Let us define

\mathcal{P}^{(t)}:=\left\{x\in\mathds{R}^{d}\,:\,u(v\log t,x)>e^{\beta(v\log t)^{\frac{6-d}{4-d}}}\right\},

for any
t>1
. Then, it suffices to show
\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}(\mathcal{P}^{(t)})\geq d
, a.s. for some
t>1
. Indeed,

\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}\left(\{t\}\times\left\{x\in\mathds{R}^{d}\,:\,u(v\log t,x)>e^{\beta(v\log t)^{\frac{6-d}{4-d}}}\right\}\right)=\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}(\mathcal{P}^{(t)}),

for any fixed
t\geq 1
(see
[
BT92
, Section 9]
). Let
t_{0}
be the constant in Theorem
6.2
. Let
\beta,v>0
. Observe that for all
(M,\alpha)\in\mathds{R}^{2}
such that

M\geq e,\quad\alpha\geq\frac{\beta(v\log t_{0})^{\frac{6-d}{4-d}}}{t_{0}(\log M)^{\frac{2}{4-d}}},

we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{(t_{0})}_{M}:=\left\{\|x\|_{\infty}\geq M\,:\,u(v\log t_{0},x)>e^{\beta(v\log t_{0})^{\frac{6-d}{4-d}}}\right\}\supseteq\left\{\|x\|_{\infty}\geq M\,:\,u(v\log t_{0},x)>e^{\alpha t_{0}(\log\|x\|_{\infty})^{\frac{2}{4-d}}}\right\}.

Note that
\mathcal{P}^{(t_{0})}_{M}
and
\mathcal{P}^{(t_{0})}
have the same macroscopic Hausdorff dimension since
\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}[E]=0
for every bounded set
E\in\mathds{R}^{d}
. Therefore, by Theorem
6.2
, we have

\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}(\mathcal{P}^{(t_{0})})=\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}(\mathcal{P}^{(t_{0})}_{M})\geq d-\frac{\beta(v\log t_{0})^{\frac{6-d}{4-d}}}{t_{0}(\log M)^{\frac{2}{4-d}}}.

By taking
M\uparrow\infty
, we can conclude that
\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}(\mathcal{P}^{(t_{0})})\geq d
, a.s.

∎

7.2.
Proof of the upper bound in Theorem
1.2

Proposition 7.3
.

Let
0<\epsilon<\beta
and
M>0
. There exist
b:=b(M)>1
and
n_{0}:=n_{0}(M,b,\epsilon)>0
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
and
t\geq 1

(7.4)

\displaystyle\mathds{P}

\displaystyle\Big(\text{ For some }t\in(a,a+l]\text{ s.t. }\sup_{x\in B(y,1)}\log u(t,x)\geq\beta t^{\frac{6-d}{4-d}},\mathfrak{a}_{0}\leq M\Big)

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}(\beta-\epsilon)^{\frac{d}{2}}a^{\frac{d}{4-d}}e^{db\log(a+l)-(1-\epsilon)\mathfrak{c}_{d}(\beta-\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}}a},

where
b>1
and
y\in\mathds{R}^{d}
.

Proof.

We use similar argument as in Proposition
6.3
. Applying Lemma
5.6
with
L_{t}:=t^{b}
, we can write
u(t,x)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{y}(t,x)
for any
y\in\mathds{R}^{d}.
Then we have

\displaystyle\mathds{P}\Big(\text{For some $t\in(a,a+l]$}\sup_{x\in B(y,1)}\log u(t,x)\geq\beta t^{\frac{6-d}{4-d}},\mathfrak{a}_{0}\leq M\Big)\leq({\bf D_{1}})+({\bf D_{2}}),

where

\displaystyle({\bf D_{1}})

\displaystyle:=\mathds{P}\Big(\text{For some $t\in(a,a+l]$}\sup_{x\in B(y,1)}\mathcal{U}_{0}^{y}(t,x)\geq\frac{1}{2}e^{\beta t^{\frac{6-d}{4-d}}},\mathfrak{a}_{0}\leq M\Big),

\displaystyle({\bf D_{2}})

\displaystyle:=\mathds{P}\Big(\text{For some $t\in(a,a+l]$}\sup_{x\in B(y,1)}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{y}(t,x)\geq\frac{1}{2}e^{\beta t^{\frac{6-d}{4-d}}},\mathfrak{a}_{0}\leq M\Big).

For
d=3
, on the event
\{\mathfrak{a}_{0}\leq M\}
, we have

\mathcal{U}^{y}_{k}(t,x)\leq C\exp\left(CtM^{\aleph+1}(b(k+1)\log t)^{2\aleph+2}-\frac{t^{2bk}}{Ct}\right).

by applying (
5.33
). For
d=2
, similar bounds follows again from Lemma 5.2 of
[
KPvZ20
]
. Now we can choose a large
b:=b(M)>0
such that for all
t\geq e
and
n\geq 1

(7.5)

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{y}(t,x)\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}C\exp\left(-C_{1}t^{2bk-1}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}e^{\alpha tn^{\frac{2}{4-d}}},

which shows that
({\bf D_{2}})=0
for all
a\geq e
. We now bound
({\bf D_{1}})
. Fix
\epsilon\in(0,\beta\wedge 1)
and use Proposition
5.5
to obtain that there exists
a_{0}:=a_{0}(b,M,\epsilon)>0
such that for all
a\geq a_{0}

(7.6)

\displaystyle({\bf D_{1}})

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}\Big(\text{For some $t\in(a,a+l]$, }C\exp(t\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{L_{t}})+CM^{\upsilon+1}(\log L_{t})^{2\upsilon+2})\geq\frac{1}{2}e^{\beta t^{\frac{6-d}{4-d}}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}\Big(\text{For some $t\in(a,a+l]$, }\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{L_{t}})\geq\beta t^{\frac{2}{4-d}}-\frac{\log(2C)+CM^{\upsilon+1}(b\log t)^{2\upsilon+2}}{t}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}(\text{For some $t\in(a,a+l]$, }\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{L_{t}})\geq(\beta-\epsilon)a^{\frac{2}{4-d}}).

By Lemma
2.2
and Lemma
2.3
, we have

(7.7)

({\bf D_{1}})\leq\mathds{P}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1}(Q^{y}_{L_{a+l}})\geq(\beta-\epsilon)a^{\frac{2}{4-d}}))\leq c_{1}(\beta-\epsilon)^{\frac{d}{2}}a^{\frac{d}{4-d}}e^{db\log(a+l)-(1-\epsilon)\mathfrak{c}_{d}(\beta-\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}}a}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Now we proceed to prove the upper bound of macroscopic Hausdorff dimension of the set
\mathcal{P}^{d}(\beta,v)
.

Theorem 7.4
.

For every
v>0
and
\beta\in(0,\left(d/(v\mathfrak{c}_{d})\right)^{\frac{2}{4-d}})
, with probability one,

(7.8)

\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}[\mathcal{P}^{d}(\beta,v)]\leq(d+1-\beta^{\frac{4-d}{2}}v\mathfrak{c}_{d})\vee d.

Proof.

For
\epsilon:=(\epsilon_{1},...,\epsilon_{d})\in\{-1,1\}^{d}
, define an (open) orthant as

\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}:=\left\{(t,x)=(t,x_{1},...,x_{d})\in(1,\infty)\times\mathds{R}^{d}\,:\,\epsilon_{1}x_{1}>0,\epsilon_{2}x_{2}>0,...,\epsilon_{d}x_{d}>0\right\}.

We then define

\mathcal{P}^{d}_{\epsilon}(\beta,v):=\mathcal{P}^{d}(\beta,v)\cap\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}.

In order to prove this theorem, it suffices to prove that
\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}[\mathcal{P}^{d}_{\epsilon_{+}}(\beta,v)](d+1-\beta^{\frac{4-d}{2}}v\mathfrak{c}_{d})\vee d
for any
\epsilon\in\{-1,1\}^{d}
. Due to symmetry between different orthants, it further suffices to prove for
\epsilon_{+}:=(1,...,1)\in\{-1,1\}^{d}
that

(7.9)

\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}[\mathcal{P}^{d}_{\epsilon_{+}}(\beta,v)]\leq(d+1-\beta^{\frac{4-d}{2}}v\mathfrak{c}_{d})\vee d.

For
q>1
and
n\in\mathds{N}
, let us denote
\mathcal{L}_{n}:=\mathcal{L}_{n}(q,n,\beta,v,d):=\mathcal{P}^{d}_{\epsilon_{+}}(\beta,v)\cap\mathcal{I}^{(q)}_{n}
where
\mathcal{I}^{(q)}_{n}:=(e^{n/q},e^{n+1}]^{d+1}
. By Lemma
B.5
, we have

\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}\left[\mathcal{P}^{d}_{\epsilon_{+}}(\beta,v)\setminus\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{L}_{n}\right]\leq d.

Since
\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}(A\cup B)=\max\{\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}(A),\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}(B)\}
for any two sets
A,B\subseteq\mathds{R}^{d}
, we have

\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}\left[\mathcal{P}^{d}_{\epsilon_{+}}(\beta,v)\right]\leq\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}\left[\mathcal{P}^{d}_{\epsilon_{+}}(\beta,v)\setminus\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{L}_{n}\right]\vee\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}\left[\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{L}_{n}\right].

Let
\bar{\mathcal{L}}:=\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{L}_{n}
. The above inequality implies that to show (
7.9
), it is enough to prove

\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}\left(\bar{\mathcal{L}}\right)\leq(d+1-\beta^{\frac{4-d}{2}}v\mathfrak{c}_{d}).

To this end, observe that Proposition
7.3
implies for all
a\in(e^{n/q},e^{n+1}]

(7.10)

\displaystyle\mathds{P}

\displaystyle\Big(\text{For some $t\in(a,a+1]$}\sup_{x\in B(y,1)}\log u(v\log t,x)\geq\beta(v\log t)^{\frac{6-d}{4-d}},\mathfrak{a}_{0}\leq M\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathds{P}(\text{For some $t\in(v\log a,v\log(a+1)]$}\sup_{x\in B(y,1)}\log u(t,x)\geq\beta t^{\frac{6-d}{4-d}},\mathfrak{a}_{0}\leq M\Big)

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}(\beta-\epsilon)^{\frac{d}{2}}\left(\frac{vn}{q}\right)^{\frac{d}{4-d}}\exp\left(db\log\left(2vn\right)-\frac{(1-\epsilon)\mathfrak{c}_{d}(\beta-\epsilon)^{\frac{4-d}{2}}vn}{q}\right),

For all sufficiently large
n\in\mathds{N}
, we cover
\mathcal{L}_{n}\subseteq\mathcal{I}^{(q)}_{n}
with
O(e^{d+1})
-many boxes of the form
(a,a+1]\times B(y,1)
satisfying that for some
t\in(a,a+1]

(7.11)

\sup_{x\in B(y,1)}\log u(v\log t,x)\geq\beta(v\log t)^{\frac{6-d}{4-d}}.

on the event
\Upsilon_{M}:=\{\mathfrak{a}_{0}\leq M\}
. Choose

\rho\in\Big(d+1-\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{d}\beta_{\epsilon}^{\frac{4-d}{2}}vn}{q},d+1\Big].

By (
7.10
), we have that for all sufficiently large
n\geq 1
and for all
\rho>0
,

\displaystyle\mathds{E}[\nu_{\rho}^{n}(\mathcal{L}_{n})\mathds{1}_{\Upsilon_{M}}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{E}\Big[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(a,a+1]\times B(y,1)\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{n}^{q}:\\
\text{\eqref{eq:covering of spatio-temporal peaks} holds}\end{subarray}}e^{-n\rho}\cdot\mathds{1}_{\Upsilon_{M}}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq C\exp\Big(\Big\{d+1-\rho-\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{d}\beta_{\epsilon}^{\frac{4-d}{2}}vn}{q}\Big\}n+Cb\log n\Big),

where
C>0
is a constant which depends only on
(v,\beta)
and
\beta_{\epsilon}:=(1-\epsilon)^{\frac{2}{4-d}}(\beta-\epsilon)
. This implies that

\mathds{P}\Big(\Upsilon_{M}\cap\Big\{\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\nu_{\rho}^{n}(\bar{\mathcal{L}})<\infty\Big\}\Big)=\mathds{P}\big(\Upsilon_{M}\big).

Because
\mathds{P}(\Upsilon_{M})\geq 1-e^{-h\sqrt{M}}\mathbb{E}[e^{\sqrt{\mathfrak{a}_{0}}}]
(see (
6.7
)), we have

\mathds{P}\Big(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\nu_{\rho}^{n}(\bar{\mathcal{L}})<\infty\Big)\geq 1-e^{-h\sqrt{M}}\mathbb{E}[e^{\sqrt{\mathfrak{a}_{0}}}],

which in turn implies

\mathds{P}(\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}(\bar{\mathcal{L}})\leq\rho)\geq 1-e^{-h\sqrt{M}}\mathbb{E}[e^{\sqrt{\mathfrak{a}_{0}}}].
Since the definition of
\bar{\mathcal{L}}
does not depend on
M
and
M>0
can be arbitrarily large, we have
\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}(\bar{\mathcal{L}})\leq\rho
almost surely. Taking
\rho\downarrow d+1-\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{d}\beta_{\epsilon}^{\frac{4-d}{2}}vn}{q}
, we also have
\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}(\bar{\mathcal{L}})\leq d+1-\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{d}\beta_{\epsilon}^{\frac{4-d}{2}}v}{q}
. Moreover, since
q>1
and
\epsilon\in(0,\beta\wedge 1)
are arbitrary, we can let
q\to 1
and
\epsilon\to 0
to conclude that
\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}(\bar{\mathcal{L}})\leq d+1-\mathfrak{c}_{d}\beta^{\frac{4-d}{2}}v
. This completes the proof.

∎

Appendix A
Besov space and paracontrolled generator

We start with introducing few notations about the function spaces and the paracontrolled calculus. Let
\chi
and
\varrho
be non-negative radial function such that

(1)

the support of
\chi
is contained in a ball and the support of
\varrho
is contained in an annulus
\{x\in\mathds{R}^{d}:1<|x|<2\}
.

(2)

\chi(\xi)+\sum_{j\geq 0}\varrho(2^{-j}\xi)=1
for all
\xi\in^{d}
.

(3)

\mathrm{Supp}(\chi)\cap\mathrm{Supp}(\varrho(2^{-j}\cdot))=\emptyset
for
i\geq 1
and
\mathrm{Supp}(\varrho(2^{-i}\cdot)\cap\mathrm{Supp}(\varrho(2^{-j}\cdot))=\emptyset
when
|i-j|>1
.

To this end,
(\chi,\varrho)
satisfying the above properties are said to form a dyadic partition of unity. For
the existence, we refer to
[
BCD11
, Proposition 2.10]
.

For any Schwartz distribution
f
, we define the Littlewood-Paley blocks by

(A.1)

\Delta_{i}f=\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}_{0}^{d}}\langle f,\mathfrak{n}_{k,L}\rangle\varrho_{i}\Big(\frac{k}{L}\Big)\mathfrak{n}_{k,L}

where
\{\mathfrak{n}_{k,L}:k\in\mathds{N}_{0}\}
forms an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}([0,L]^{d})
given in
[
CvZ21
, Section 4]
and
\varrho_{j}(\cdot)=\varrho(2^{-j}\cdot)
. We also note that since
\varrho_{j}
is supported in a ball with radius
2^{j}
and
\varrho_{j}\leq 1
, for all
j\in\mathds{N}_{0}
,
x\in\mathds{R}^{d}
and
\gamma>0

(A.2)

\varrho_{j}(x)\lesssim\left(\frac{2^{j}}{1+|x|}\right)^{\gamma}.

For
u\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
, we define
(1-\frac{1}{2}\Delta)^{-1}u
by

(A.3)

(1-\frac{1}{2}\Delta)^{-1}u:=\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}^{d}_{0}}\sigma\Big(\frac{k}{L}\Big)\langle u,\mathfrak{n}_{k,L}\rangle\mathfrak{n}_{k,L},

where
\sigma(x):=(1+\pi|x|^{2})^{-1}
.

Denote the Fourier transform operator by
\mathfrak{F}
and let
(\chi,\varrho)
be the dyadic partition of unity. Then the Littlewood-Paley blocks are defined as

(A.4)

\displaystyle\Delta_{-1}u=\mathfrak{F}^{-1}(\chi\mathfrak{F}(u)),\quad\Delta_{j}u=\mathfrak{F}^{-1}\big(\varrho_{j}(\cdot)\mathfrak{F}(u)\big),\quad j\geq 0

where
\varrho_{j}(\cdot)=\varrho(2^{-j}\cdot)
and, for
\alpha\in
,
p,q\in[1,\infty]
, the Besov space
B^{\alpha}_{p,q}(^{d},^{n})
is

(A.5)

\displaystyle B^{\alpha}_{p,q}(^{d},^{n}):=\{u\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(^{d},^{n});\quad\|u\|^{q}_{B^{\alpha}_{p,q}(^{d},^{n})}=\sum_{j\geq-1}2^{jq\alpha}\|\Delta_{j}u\|^{q}_{L^{p}(^{d},^{n})}<\infty\}

We often use the notation
\mathscr{C}_{p}^{\alpha}(^{d},^{n})
to denote
B^{\alpha}_{p,p}(^{d},^{n})
for
p\in[1,\infty]
and write
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(^{d},^{n})
for
\mathscr{C}_{\infty}^{\alpha}(^{d},^{n})
. This notation is consistent with the fact that
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(^{d},^{n})
is indeed space of all
\alpha
-Hölder continuous function. For simplicity, we sometimes use the notation
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{p}
for
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{p}(\mathds{R}^{d},\mathds{R})
.

Let
\delta,\rho>0
,
T>0,
and
\bar{T}\in[0,T)
. Let
(D,\|\cdot\|_{D})
be a Banach space and
u,v:[T-\bar{T},T]\rightarrow D
be function (or distribution) valued processes. We say that
u\in C^{\delta}_{\rho,\bar{T},T}D
if
\|u\|_{C^{\delta}_{\rho,\bar{T},T}D}<\infty
and
v\in C^{\delta}_{\bar{T},T}D
if
\|v\|_{C^{\delta}_{\bar{T},T}D}<\infty
where

(A.6)

\displaystyle\|u\|_{C^{\delta}_{\rho,\bar{T},T}D}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{s<t\in(T-\bar{T},T]}(T-t)^{\delta}\frac{\|u(t)-u(s)\|_{D}}{|t-s|^{\rho}},

\displaystyle\|v\|_{C^{\delta}_{\bar{T},T}D}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{t\in(T-\bar{T},T]}(T-t)^{\delta}\|v(t)\|_{D}.

In the case of
\delta=0
or
\bar{T}=T
, then we drop the respective subscripts in the above definition.

A.1.
Some properties of the Besov-Hölder continuous distributions

Let
f
and
g
be two distributions in
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(^{d})
. Then the Paley-Littlewood decomposition of
fg
is written as

\displaystyle fg=f\prec g+f\circ g+f\succ g

where
f\prec g
and
f\succ g
are called
paraproducts
and
f\circ g
is called the
resonant terms
and they are defined as

\displaystyle f\prec g=f\succ g=\sum_{j\geq-1}\sum_{i<j-1}\Delta_{i}\Delta_{j}g,\quad\text{and }\quad f\circ g=\sum_{j\geq-1}\sum_{|i-j|\leq 1}\Delta_{i}\Delta_{j}g.

In the following propositions, we note few useful properties of the paraproduct.

Proposition A.1

(Bony’s estimates (I),
[
BCD11
]
)
.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in\mathds{R}
. Let
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
and
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
,

(1)

If
\alpha>0
, then
f\prec g\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
and
\|f\prec g\|_{\beta}\leq\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}\|g\|_{\beta}

(2)

If
\alpha<0
, then
f\prec g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\beta}
and
\|f\prec g\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\alpha}\|g\|_{\beta}
.

(3)

If
\alpha+\beta>0
, then
f\circ g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\beta}
and
\|f\circ g\|_{\alpha+\beta}\lesssim\|f\|_{\alpha}\|g\|_{\beta}
.

Proposition A.2

(Bony’s estimates (II),
[
BCD11
]
)
.

Let
\alpha<0,\beta>0
and
\alpha+\beta>0
. Let
p,p_{1},p_{2},q_{1},q_{2}\in[1,\infty]
be satisfy
\frac{1}{p}=\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\frac{1}{p_{2}}
. Let
f\in B^{\alpha}_{p_{1},q_{1}}
and
g\in B^{\beta}_{p_{2},q_{2}}
. For
q\geq q_{1}

(1)

\|f\prec g\|_{B^{\alpha+\beta}_{p,q}}\lesssim\|f\|_{B^{\alpha}_{p_{1},q_{1}}}\|g\|_{B^{\beta}_{p_{2},q_{2}}}
.

(2)

\|f\succ g\|_{B^{\alpha}_{p,q}}\lesssim\|f\|_{B^{\alpha}_{p_{1},q_{1}}}\|g\|_{B^{\beta}_{p_{2},q_{2}}}.

(3)

\|f\circ g\|_{B^{\alpha+\beta}_{p,q}}\lesssim\|f\|_{B^{\alpha}_{p_{1},q_{1}}}\|g\|_{B^{\beta}_{p_{2},q_{2}}}.

Proposition A.3

(Schauder’s estimate,Lemma 2.5 of
[
CC18b
]
, Lemma A.8 of
[
GIP15a
]
)
.

Let
P_{t}
be the heat semigroup for
\frac{1}{2}\Delta
. Let
\theta\geq 0,p\in[1,\infty]
and
\alpha\in\mathds{R}
. Then for
\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{p}
and
0\leq s\leq t
we have

(A.7)

\|P_{t}\phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+2\theta}_{p}}\lesssim t^{-\theta}\|\phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{p}},\qquad\|(P_{t-s}-\textrm{Id})\phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-2\theta}_{p}}\lesssim|t-s|^{\theta}\|\phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{p}}.

Appendix B
Macroscopic Hausdorff dimension

In this section, we introduce the notion of the macroscopic Hausdorff dimension given by Barlow and Taylor
[
BT89
,
BT92
]
, and Khoshnevisan-Kim-Xiao
[
KKX17
]
. We also present a useful propositions that help to provide lower bound and upper bounds to the macroscopic Hausdorff dimension of any given set.

B.1.
Definition

For all integers
n\geq 1
, we define the exponential cubes and shells as follows:

(B.1)

\mathds{V}_{n}:=[-e^{n},e^{n})^{d},\quad\mathds{S}_{0}:=V_{0},\quad\text{and}\quad\mathds{S}_{n+1}:=\mathds{V}_{n+1}\setminus\mathds{V}_{n}.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be the collection of all cubes of the form

(B.2)

B(x,r):=\prod_{i=1}^{d}[x_{i},x_{i}+r),

for
x=(x_{1},...,x_{d})\in\mathds{R}^{d}
, and
r\in[1,\infty)
. For any subset
E\subset\mathds{R}^{d}
,
\rho>0
, and all integers
n\geq 1
, we define

(B.3)

\nu_{\rho}^{n}(E):=\inf\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left(\frac{s(B_{i})}{e^{n}}\right)^{\rho}:B_{i}\in\mathcal{B},B_{i}\subset\mathds{S}_{n}\text{ and }E\cap\mathds{S}_{n}\subset\cup_{i=1}^{m}B_{i}\right\},

where
s(B):=r
denotes the side of
B=B(x,r)
. We now introduce the definition of the macroscopic Hausdorff dimension.

Definition B.1
.

[
[
BT89
,
BT92
]
]
The
macroscopic Hausdorff dimension
of
E\subset\mathds{R}^{d}
is defined as

(B.4)

\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}(E):=\inf\left\{\rho>0:\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\nu_{\rho}^{n}(E)<\infty\right\}.

B.2.
Useful bounds for macroscopic Hausdorff dimension

Choose and fix any
\theta\in(0,1)
. We define

a_{j,n}(\theta):=e^{n}+je^{n\theta},\qquad 0\leq j<e^{n(1-\theta)},

I_{n}(\theta):=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq j\leq e^{n(1-\theta)}:\\
j\in\mathds{Z}\end{subarray}}\{a_{j,n}(\theta)\},

and

\mathcal{I}_{n}(\theta):=\prod_{i=1}^{d}I^{i}_{n}(\theta),

where
I^{i}_{n}(\theta)
is a copy of
I_{n}(\theta)
for each
i
. We call
\cup_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{I}_{n}(\theta)
a
\theta
-skeleton of
\mathds{R}^{d}
(see
[
KKX17
, Definition 4.2]
). Note that
\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}\left(\cup_{n=k}^{\infty}\mathcal{I}_{n}(\theta)\right)=d(1-\theta)
for any integer
k\geq 1
.

Definition B.2

(Definition 4.3 of
[
KKX17
]
)
.

E
is called
\theta
-thick if there exists a positive integer
k=k(\theta)
such that

E\cap Q(x,e^{n\theta})\neq\emptyset,

for all
x\in\mathcal{I}_{n}(\theta)
and
n\geq k
.

By the monotonicity of the macroscopic Hausdorff dimension, we get the following lower bound.

Proposition B.3

(Proposition 4.4 of
[
KKX17
]
)
.

Let
E\subset\mathds{R}^{d}
. If
E
contains a
\theta
-thick set for some
\theta\in(0,1)
, then

\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}(E)\geq d(1-\theta).

For the set of spatio-temporal peaks, we separately provide a proposition for the lower bound of the macroscopic Hausdorff dimension.

Proposition B.4

(Theorem 4.1 of
[
BT92
]
)
.

Fix
\gamma\in(0,d)
. For any set
E
and
n\in\mathds{Z}
, let us define

(B.5)

\displaystyle\mu_{n}(E)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\mathds{Z}\\
e^{n}<s\leq e^{n+1}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vec{j}\in\mathds{Z}^{d}\\
\vec{j}\in[0,e^{n(1-\gamma)})\end{subarray}}\mathds{1}\{(s,j)\in E\}.

Then, there exists a constant
C>0
such that
\nu_{n,d+1-d\gamma}(E)\geq Ce^{-nd(1-\gamma)-n}\mu_{n}(E)
.

Proof.

The proof follows from
[
BT92
, Theorem 4.1]
. For the condition of
[
BT92
, Theorem 4.1]
, it suffices to verify that
\mu_{n}((s,s+r]\times B(x,r))\lesssim r^{d+1}
for all
r\geq 1
, which is proven below (4.24) of
[
Yi22
]
.
∎

The following lemma helps us to compute the upper bound of the macroscopic Hausdorff dimension.

Lemma B.5

(Lemma 4.2 of
[
Yi22
]
)
.

For any
q>1
and
k\in\{1,...,d-1\}
, define a set
E\subseteq\mathds{R}^{d}
as

E:=\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}E_{n},

where

E_{n}:=(0,e^{n/q}]^{k}\times(e^{n/q},e^{n+1}]^{d-k}.

Then we have
\mathrm{Dim}_{\mathds{H}}[E]\leq d-k.
